To fix (3)

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 1 imprecise p value out of 2 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
p < 0.05 Relative to a fixed per-edge threshold (e.g., p < 0.05), this approach adapts the rejection boundary to the global distribution of p-values and controls the expected proportion of false discoveries among retained edges, typically yielding greater power while maintaining a principled error rate (Benjamini & Yekutieli, 2001).

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

README Files

README files are a way to document the contents and structure of a folder, helping users locate the information they need. You can use a README to document changes to a repository, and explain how files are named. Please consider adding a README to each repository or including ‘README’ in the name of your overview document.

Unclassified Files

We could not classify 2 files by name or extension: .Rbuildignore, CRAN-SUBMISSION. Add a recognisable keyword (data, code, materials, documentation, output) to the file name, or use a common extension, so both humans and machines can tell what kind of file it is.

File Naming

File names should be machine-parseable: no spaces or special characters, and every file should be classifiable by name or extension. We found 9 naming problems that should be fixed, and 9 files with a naming suggestion (not required, but a good habit).

BrJSocPsychol_2024_plot_9.pdf zero-padding Suggestion numbered inconsistently with sibling files (pad to 2 digits so names sort in numeric order)

Files that do not belong in the repository

We left out 1 file that software creates by itself, not the authors: operating system (1). They hold nothing a reader can use, but they clutter the repository, and software can mistake them for real files (for example a ._ copy of a .csv for data).

Remove these: Operating system files: macOS adds a __MACOSX folder and a ._ copy of every file it puts in a zip, and .DS_Store files to folders; Windows adds Thumbs.db and desktop.ini. On a Mac, create zips from the command line with zip -r -X, or remove them afterwards with zip -d archive.zip "__MACOSX/*" "*.DS_Store". RStudio session files (.Rproj.user/) hold open tabs and unsaved edits; R history (.Rhistory) lists the commands typed in a session and can hold file paths and passwords; Jupyter checkpoints (.ipynb_checkpoints/) are automatic copies of notebooks; compiled Python files (__pycache__/, .pyc) are recreated when the code runs; open-document lock files (~$...docx, .~lock...#) are left while a document is open; editor backups (file~, .bak, .swp) and temporary files (.tmp, .temp) are copies a program made by itself.

Keep only on purpose: version control folders (.git, .svn, .hg) hold the project’s history, which can include earlier versions of files and data that was later removed; editor settings (.idea, .vscode) can hold paths on your own computer. A .gitignore based on GitHub’s templates (https://github.com/github/gitignore) keeps them out of a repository.

Code Comments

Missing Files

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. No absolute file paths were found in any of the code files.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). install.packages() calls were found in 6 code files.

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

We did not detect an ethics approval statement, and this paper does not appear to involve live data collection from human or animal participants. Ethics approval is typically not required for theoretical work, simulations, reviews, meta-analyses, or secondary analyses of published data.

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 23 columns across 3 data files.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 3 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

Replication Check

We checked 45 references with DOIs. No citations to articles in the FLoRA database were found.

How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited (currently, a warning is provided regardless of whether the replication/reproduction study is already cited).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

This tutorial introduces ResIN, a package for the R programming language designed to estimate, visualize, and analyze Response-Item Networks (ResIN ). ResIN is an increasingly popular method for exploring complex attitude systems as sparse, spatially interpretable networks. The package simplifies and unifies the underlying workflow while offering a range of convenience features, including network plotting, community detection, individual-level score extraction, bootstrap-based uncertainty estimation, and the export of ResIN objects to other statistical software environments. Using political attitudes as an example, we provide an overview of the ResIN method, its computational implementation in R, and demonstrate the utility of key package features through an applied replication tutorial based on a recent study by Lüders et al., 2024. The paper concludes by briefly situating the ResIN method and package among several conceptually and methodologically related belief network models currently available in R.

1 Introduction

The study of complex socio-political attitude systems is currently booming across the social and behavioral sciences (e.g., Bakker & Lelkes, 2022; Baldassarri & Goldberg, 2014; Bertero et al., 2024; Boutyline, 2017; Boutyline & Vaisey, 2017; Brandt, 2022; Brandt et al., 2019; DellaPosta, 2020; Fishman & Davis, 2022; Van Noord et al., 2025; Zwicker et al., 2020). Arguably, statistical belief network models (BNMs) capture the inherent complexity of such systems more faithfully than many conventional latent scaling and dimensionality reduction techniques (Brandt & Sleegers, 2021; Dalege et al., 2016; Quayle, 2025; Warncke, 2025), allowing researchers to explore macro-and component-level properties in much richer detail whilst avoiding taxing and often unrealistic assumptions that conventional statistics impose on such systems (e.g., Chen et al., 2025; Quayle, 2025; Warncke et al., 2026).

Response-Item Networks (ResIN ) constitute a novel class of BNM that combines statistical network modeling with the spatial interpretability of established dimensionality-reduction methods (Carpentras et al., 2024). In a nutshell, ResIN locates each possible response option across survey items (e.g., strongly agree, somewhat agree, neutral, somewhat disagree, strongly disagree) within a theoretically interpretable latent space. When a given pair of attitudes is frequently co-endorsed, such as anti-abortion and anti-gun-control positions among the US electorate, ResIN maps them closely together. By contrast, attitude pairings that are rarely co-endorsed, such as anti-abortion and pro-gun-control positions, appear farther apart in ResIN -space. By simultaneously modeling patterns of relative attraction and repulsion across all possible choice pairings, ResIN recovers the principal axes of agreement and division within attitude networks.

Furthermore, ResIN provides a more granular angle of investigation than extant BNMs because it generates belief networks at the item-response level. 1 The method can thus uncover complex interactions between-and asymmetries among-attitude clusters associated with supporters of different political parties, social groups, or strata. Several recent studies demonstrate the potential of this approach, including on vaccination uptake (Carpentras et al., 2022), ideological cue taking (Lüders et al., 2024), and comparative belief systems structure (Chen et al., 2025; Warncke et al., 2026). 1 In the context of quantitative survey research, issue items typically refer to close-ended questions probing for respondents' positions on particular issues, such as the minimum wage, abortion, and CO2 emissions. Item responses denote endorsed answer choices to such questions. For instance, "strongly opposing cuts to the minimum wage", "favoring restrictions to abortion" or "being neutral on carbon tax emissions" are examples of item responses to the above listed items. This paper introduces ResIN, a new software package for estimating, visualizing, and interpreting ResIN graphs in R. In Section 2, we begin with a theoretical and mathematical overview of the ResIN method. This is followed by a discussion of the R-package design philosophy in Section 3.1. Next, we demonstrate how to install and test the package and illustrate the basic utility and output of the flagship ResIN() function in Section 3.2. A more comprehensive workflow tutorial in Section 3.3 replicates the main findings of a recent study by Lüders et al., 2024. This tutorial covers the use of node-level covariates-that is, core demographic and psychological characteristics of respondents who endorse particular issue positions (Section 3.3.1)-as well as individual-level position scores along the dominant axis of ResIN graphs (Section 3.3.2) and the use of the ResIN bootstrap functions to quantify statistical uncertainty around network estimates (Section 3.3.3).

We cover more advanced capabilities of the package in the remainder of the applied tutorial component of this manuscript. These include edge, node-, and graph-level descriptive statistics (Section 3.4), network cluster detection (Section 3.5), network pruning (Section 3.6), modeling multimodal ResIN -networks (Section 3.7), and exporting ResIN-objects to other statistical environments within R, Python, Julia, and Gephi (Section 3.8).

In Section 4, we briefly situate ResIN within a rapidly expanding family of statistical belief network models, hoping to provide readers with elementary guidance about the method's overall scope, its relative strengths, and its shortcomings compared to other conceptually and computationally related alternatives available in R. Final Section 5 discusses some important limitations users should be aware of when working with ResIN, while offering practical advice and ideas about future conceptual advances in the method.

2 The ResIN method

2.1 Theoretical background and rationale

ResIN provides a model of co-endorsement patterns contained within positional attitude systems (Carpentras et al., 2024). Broadly understood, positional attitudes denote valenced sentiments towards socially or politically relevant issues. For instance, opposing cuts to the minimum wage, being neutral on carbon taxes, or supporting equal pay for women and men constitute positional attitudes (e.g., Hatemi & McDermott, 2016). If such attitudes are socially shared, they develop links within collective belief-systems and thus serve as definable and recognizable attributes both of groupness and social selfhood (O'Reilly et al., 2024; Quayle, 2025). ResIN presumes that the more a given pair of attitudes (e.g., simultaneously supporting equal pay for men and women while opposing cuts to the minimum wage) is socially co-endorsed, the more closely this pair is located within a latent attitude space (Carpentras et al., 2024). Spatial proximity between any node pair in ResIN can be interpreted as abstract (or ideological) distance between those item-responses, or equivalently, as social distance between respondents within the latent space (Quayle, 2025).

ResIN models all possible pairwise interactions among a given set of attitudes within a single statistical network. In ResIN -networks, attitude responses form the nodes while the edges determine the associative strength between them. The method assumes that only sufficiently co-endorsed (i.e., positively correlated) node pairs attract one another. At the same time, all attitude nodes experience a constant repulsive force. The core of the ResIN method involves a computational simulation that balances these opposing forces, relying on a force-directed network plotting algorithm (Fruchterman & Reingold, 1991), which yields a spatially interpretable, theoretically meaningful latent structure in which both attitudes and people can be co-located (Carpentras et al., 2024).

We next describe the main computational steps involved in ResIN ; more comprehensive and applied introductions to the method itself, including detailed theoretical and practical reasons behind each step, can be found in Carpentras et al., 2024 and Warncke et al., 2026.

2.2 Data transformation and correlation estimation

The first step in ResIN involves transforming an n × K data table, X, where n equals the number of respondents and K the number of available (survey) items. Throughout, we index respondents by p ∈ {1, . . . , n}, survey items by k ∈ {1, . . . , K}, and the response options belonging to a given item by r. Each cell (p, k) of X thus contains the response provided by respondent p to item k, which we denote X [p, k].

From X, a new table, Y, is obtained by expanding every response level contained in X into a separate column of binary data (a process also known as dummy-and one-hot-coding). Each new column within the expanded data frame is set to 1 if a given response was endorsed, and 0 otherwise. More specifically, let R k = {R k,1 , . . . , R k,J k } denote the set of response options available for item k, where J k = |R k | and R k,r is the r-th such option, with1 ≤ r ≤ J k . For each item k, ResIN produces J k new columns, d k,1 , . . . , d k,J k , so that every column of Y is uniquely identified by the item-response pair (k, r). The entry of column d k,r for respondent p can then be written as:

Table Y is therefore of dimension n × s, with s = K k=1 J k . Expression (2) provides an example thereof.

Next, a sparse, s × s pairwise-correlation table Z is obtained from Y by correlating every pair of dummy-coded columns that stem from different original items. Writing the second item-response pair as (l, m), the entry of Z corresponding to columns d k,r and d l,m is

By default, ResIN relies on the Pearson correlation but the method also works with tetrachoric correlations (both approaches are implemented in the ResIN package). 2 An example is provided in the following expression (4). 2 The basic choice between Pearson and tetrachoric correlations is governed by a closely related consideration. For two binary indicators the Pearson correlation reduces to the ϕ coefficient, whose attainable maximum is a function of the marginal endorsement rates: for options endorsed by proportions π1 ≤ π2, ϕ cannot exceed π1(1 -π2)/[π2(1 -π1)], which falls well below unity whenever the two marginals are dissimilar. Two strongly associated positions endorsed by 10 and 50 per cent of the sample, respectively, can attain a ϕ of at most 0.33. Since ResIN edge weights determine attraction strength in the layout, this attenuation is not always innocuous as it systematically shrinks the edges of unpopular response options and thereby displaces them toward the periphery of the latent space. Tetrachoric correlations remove the dependence on marginals by positing a bivariate normal latent continuum underlying each pair of dichotomies, and are advisable whenever endorsement rates vary substantially across the response set-the typical situation with skewed or strongly polarized items. However, this correction does not come for free: tetrachorics carry larger standard errors than ϕ, and they can become unstable or undefined when a cell of the 2 × 2 table is empty or near-empty.

Finally, the algorithm obtains the positive, undirected, weighted adjacency matrix Z ′ by clipping all negative entries in Z to zero. This step is necessary as negative edges cannot be processed by conventional force-directed network plotting algorithms. Carpentras et al., 2024 have shown that setting correlation pairs to zero results in no noticeable loss in precision in the estimation of node locations in a structured latent space. Equation ( 5) gives the general rule, where Z ab denotes the entry in row a and column b of Z under any fixed ordering a, b ∈ {1, . . . , s} of the dummy columns, while Equation ( 6) performs the thresholding on an example.

2.3 Force-directed algorithm

After obtaining the positive entry-only correlation matrix, a force-directed network layout algorithm is applied to determine the two-dimensional position of each response node. By default, ResIN relies on the algorithm of Fruchterman and Reingold (1991), which treats nodes as negatively charged particles connected by Hooke-like springs. While all nodes repel each other, the edges retained in Z ′ pull connected nodes back together with a strength proportional to the correlation weight. Consequently, strongly correlated response nodes appear closer together while tenuously (and un-)connected pairs drift further apart. (Appendix Equations ( 9)-( 14) provide full details on the Fruchterman and Reingold (1991) algorithm as implemented in ResIN.)

Thus, Z ′ is mapped onto a 2 × s coordinate matrix, C, providing the (x, y) coordinates of each item-response node once the force-directed layout algorithm has converged. We illustrate this step with hypothetical coordinate values in Equation ( 7):

2.4 Coordinate rotation

The final step in ResIN involves a principal components (PC) rotation of the node coordinate matrix, which ensures the spatial interpretability of the dominant latent dimension of a particular attitude system. We mathematically describe this procedure in Appendix A.2. In a nutshell, the PC rotation preserves all pairwise distances while removing the arbitrary rotation obtained from the force-directed network plotting algorithm. This results in a more interpretable embedding whose x-and y-axes correspond to the first and second principal components of shared variance among the attitude node locations. Expression (8) illustrates that variance along the 3 We emphasize that the restriction to two dimensions is a visualization and interpretability choice in the current computational implementation of the ResIN method rather than the outcome of a formal dimensionality criterion.

ResIN embeds response nodes in the plane because a two-dimensional layout can be read directly off the page and because the dominant axis is ordinarily the quantity of substantive interest; the underlying force-directed algorithm generalizes to higher dimensions, although the present implementation returns a twodimensional layout by default. No formal criterion establishes that two dimensions are optimal for visualizing or spatially situating a given attitude system. However, one practical benefit of the PC rotation is that the axes are ordered by the variance they capture, so users can gauge how much of the layout's structure is carried by the second dimension relative to the first. Researchers for whom the dimensionality of an attitude system is itself the object of inquiry should address that question with a method designed for it, such as EGA (Section 4).

2.5 Network plotting and investigation

The rotated coordinate matrix C ′ provides the two-dimensional position of every response node and thereby defines the spatial embedding of the ResIN graph. More specifically, each column of C ′ corresponds to one response option d k,r and becomes a node in the plotted network, while the non-zero entries of the weighted adjacency matrix Z ′ determine which pairs of nodes are connected by edges and how strongly they attract one another in the visualization. Thus, C ′ supplies the node coordinates, whereas Z ′ supplies the edge structure and edge weights.

In practice, the resulting graph can be visualized by placing each response node d k,r at its rotated coordinates (x k,r , y k,r ) and drawing weighted, undirected edges between all pairs of nodes for which Z ′ ab > 0. The resulting network representation preserves the spatial logic recovered by the force-directed layout and subsequent principal-components rotation: response options that are frequently co-endorsed tend to appear close together, whereas more weakly associated or unconnected responses appear farther apart. Figure 1 provides a schematic example.

Figure 1: Schematic illustration of how the rotated coordinate matrix C ′ and weighted adjacency matrix Z ′ jointly determine a ResIN plot. Columns of C ′ become response nodes d k,r positioned at (x k,r , y k,r ), while non-zero entries in Z ′ determine the weighted edges between them.

Once plotted, ResIN graphs can be inspected both visually and statistically for substantive structure. Informative features typically include the emergence of clusters or communities of mutually compatible attitudes, asymmetries in the spatial arrangement of issue positions, and differences in node centrality that may indicate especially influential or representative response options within the broader attitude system. We return to descriptive and inferential tools for each of these aspects in greater detail below.

3 The ResIN package for R

3.1 Design philosophy

Following a "simple by default, flexible when needed" approach, the ResIN package was crafted around ResIN::ResIN() as a single, flagship utility function.

Since ResIN's core user base largely consists of social scientists with limited to intermediate statistical programming skills, we wrote ResIN() to work 'out-of-the-box' for the most common belief network research and analysis tasks. To be fully operable, ResIN() thus solely requires a user-supplied survey dataset via the df argument. At the same time, the function exposes a high degree of argument-based configurability, allowing users to tailor correlation estimation, missing-data handling, pruning decisions, plotting behavior, and downstream network-analytic steps to their specific needs.

To facilitate transparent and iterative workflows, ResIN() also stores important, intermediate products of the estimation pipeline covered across Equations ( 4), (7), and (8) (e.g., the dummy-coded response matrix, network adjacency matrix, cluster assignment quantities, and additional auxiliary objects), enabling more advanced users to inspect intermediate results, extract particular components for further analyses, and refine their modeling choices as needed.

Beyond implementing the ResIN -method, the ResIN() function thus unifies several routine belief network analysis tasks under a single software umbrella. The function particularly streamlines network plotting workflows by automating data reprocessing and supplying several sensible default parameters to ggplot2 as primary plotting engine (Wickham et al., 2019).

Besides calculating various network, node, and edge-level statistics, ResIN() can also perform basic statistical inference tasks about node-level co-variates, detect network clusters, and estimate psychometric-type scores for survey respondents based on attitude node locations within the ResIN layout. These capabilities can be activated by user-defined arguments which will be covered in more detail from Section 3.3 onward.

The principles of interoperability and replicability further guided the package design. By default, ResIN() returns a self-contained, classed object that stores core results (edge-and node tables, adjacency matrix, plots, and auxiliary objects) alongside reproducibility metadata (e.g., package version, call, and input identifiers). Moreover, the package ships with explicit coercion and export methods that allow users to port ResIN-objects to other, commonly used graph ecosystems without manual reformatting. In particular, the as.igraph() and as.qgraph() methods enable immediate integration with the igraph (Csardi & Nepusz, 2006) and qgraph (Epskamp et al., 2012) toolchains, while as.network() supports workflows in the statnet ecosystem (Handcock et al., 2019). Finally, lightweight cross-language exporters facilitate reproducible downstream analysis beyond R: as.networkx() and as.graphsjl() generate edge-and node tables that can seamlessly be re-imported into NetworkX in Python and Graphs.jl in Julia, while as.gephi() writes Gephi-readable edge-(and optional node-) tables for external visualization and inspection (Hogervorst, 2022). Each of these methods preserves all node-and edge-level attributes obtained with ResIN as tabular metadata. The task of deriving uncertainty estimates for network quantities can easily lead to computational and programming bottlenecks. This is why ResIN ships with three sequential helper functions which are designed to simplify the input, execution, and results extraction tasks by compartmentalizing each step. First, the ResIN_boots_prepare() function specifies which network object(s) and properties users wish to resample. Next, ResIN_boots_execute() handles repeated network estimation while providing optional back-end utilities for CPU-parallelism. Finally, ResIN_boots_ extract() provides a forward-search and concatenate utility, helping users to extract and obtain statistical summaries from their target quantities of interest. We cover the basic usage of the ResIN-bootstrap functions in Section 3.3.3. Figure 2 summarizes the main functions and functionalities of the package. In Appendix A.3, we provide more detail on the computational implementation of the ResIN() function and the packaged bootstrapping utilities, including other R packages ResIN depends on. ➢ Network estimation ➢ Visualization ➢ Co-variate analysis ➢ Score extraction ➢ Descriptive statistics ➢ Cluster detection ➢ Network pruning ➢ Multimodal networks Bootstrapping 1) ResIN_boots_prepare 2) ResIN_boots_execute 3) ResIN_boots_extract Export & conversion as.igraph() as.qgraph() as.network () as.gephi() as.networkx() as.graphjl() ResIN() function

3.2 Package installation, basic usage, and output

In the tutorial that follows, readers can follow along by copying and pasting code into their R command interface. Alternatively, the full script is provided as part of the online supplementary material accessible here: https://doi.org/10.7910/DVN/WQLSIP.

The package can be installed from https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/ResIN/index.html and loaded by: if(! require('ResIN')) {install.packages('ResIN')} library('ResIN') Or alternatively, via GitHub and devtools: if(! require('devtools')) {install.packages('devtools')} devtools::install_github('pwarncke77/ResIN') To test a basic network model, users can rely on a simulated, Likert-type data object, called lik_data that ships with the package. This dataset was generated to mimic political issue position data captured in the 2020 wave of the American National Election Studies (ANES) (American National Election Studies, 2021). The ResIN() function performs all data transformation and computational operations to estimate and visualize ResIN graphs. By default, it also outputs a basic plot of the estimated network, such as the one shown in Figure 3. As ResIN() relies on ggplot2 for all plotting capabilities, resulting network plots can be further inspected and processed as native ggplotclass objects (which are stored in ResIN_out$ResIN_ggplot). 4 ResIN_out <-ResIN::ResIN(lik_data) Figure 3: Default ResIN plot generated by ggplot2. The automatic display of the default plot output can be suppressed by setting the parameter plot_ggplot to FALSE when calling the ResIN function, as follows: ResIN_out <-ResIN::ResIN(lik_data, plot_ggplot = FALSE) The output list generated by ResIN() consists of six further elements. The first, ResIN_ edgelist, is an edge-list type data matrix with rows equal to the number of non-zero elements retained in the ResIN adjacency matrix Z ′ (see Equation 6). In addition to edge weights and spatial coordinates, ResIN_edgelist contains edge-level network statistics which will be further discussed in Section 3.4. Next in line is a corresponding node-level data-frame, called ResIN_nodeframe, that, like ResIN_edgelist, details the spatial coordinates for each node, along with node-level co-variate and centrality statistics. By default, calling the S3 method as.data.frame() on a ResIN output list will return this node-level data-frame (a behavior that can be superseded by specifying which="edges" or which="scores" inside the as.data.frame()-call). The generated network plot object appears underneath as ResIN_ggplot-a ggplot-native list-object. str(ResIN_out, max.level = 1, vec.len = 0, give.attr = FALSE, list.len = length(ResIN_out)) List of 7 $ ResIN_edgelist :'data.frame': 570 obs. of 9 variables: $ ResIN_nodeframe :'data.frame': 60 obs. of 8 variables: $ ResIN_ggplot :List of 11 $ ResIN_scores :'data.frame': 1000 obs. of 6 variables: $ graph_stats :List of 2 $ aux_objects :List of 6 $ multimodal_output: chr ... The aux_objects-list contains an amalgam of intermediate solutions obtained as part of the main ResIN algorithm. For instance, users can access the dummy-coded response data frame, Y, via aux_objects [["df_dummies"]] and adjacency matrix Z ′ by referencing aux_objects [["adj_ matrix"]]. Finally, a list-object called multimodal_output, which contains a multimodal network solution in which both rows (usually respondents) and columns (usually response items) enter as nodes, will optionally be populated if the parameter multimodal=TRUE. (This type of graph is further discussed in Section 3.7).

3.3 Workflow tutorial based on Lüders et al. 2024

In this section, we illustrate the basic usage of the ResIN package with a complete workflow tutorial, covering basic data cleaning and pre-processing, network estimation, interpretation, and statistical inference tasks typically involved in ResIN analyses. Our example is based on a replication of Lüders et al., 2024-'Attitude networks as intergroup realities: Using network-modeling to research attitude-identity relationships in polarized political contexts'. Lüders et al., 2024 use ResIN to investigate whether co-endorsement patterns among political attitudes reveal meaningful attitude-identity relationships using the United States of America as a case study of mass political polarization. The central expectation is that the latent structure of political attitudes should align with partisan identities and thereby reveal which issues are perceived to "belong" to opposing political camps. The authors find that the estimated attitude network recovers two conflictive partisan belief systems; the spatial location of response nodes carries substantial information about latent partisan identity. Both findings imply that attitude networks can be used not only to summarize opinion structure, but also to study how attitudes function as socially meaningful cues in intergroup perception and identity management.

The original authors agreed to publish their primary data-set as part of ResIN-package version 2.0.0+ as a dedicated data object that is accessible via ResIN::BrJSocPsychol_2024 or by calling data(BrJSocPsychol_2024). The dataset consists of eight, Likert-type political attitude questions fielded to a nationally diverse sample of 402 US adults in 2020. The attitudes cover positions on the legality of abortion, income inequality, illegal migration, welfare spending, gay marriage, environmental protection, gun control, and aid to African Americans.

We commence by loading the original data and selecting the same attitude variables as Lüders et al., 2024. We further apply more informative variable and response option labels with tidyverse functions (Wickham et al., 2019).

colnames(Core_Items) <-c("legal_abort", "equalize_incomes", "keep_immigrants", "welfare_spending", "gay_marriage", "protect_environ", "gun_control", "aid_blacks")

To further enhance network visualization, we recommend replacing verbal, Likert-style agreement/disagreement categories with informative symbols (e.g., ++, +, +/-, -, --), as demonstrated in the following code snippet.

Core_Items <-Core_Items %>% mutate(across(everything(), ~dplyr::recode(., "Strongly Agree" = "++", "Somewhat Agree" = "+", "Neutral" = "+/-", "Somewhat Disagree" = "-", "Strongly Disagree" = "--" ))) head(Core_Items) Here, we opt to set strong agreement with legalizing abortion as left-hand anchoring node by setting the left_anchor argument to "legal_abort_++". 5We further enhance the visual output through differential coloring of individual response choices by setting plot_whichstat="choices" and by specifying the Likert-type response labels for the color legend by setting the response_levels to match the above defined symbols. Setting responselabel_repel=TRUE ensures less overlap between printed node labels, the behavior of which can be further fine-tuned with the responselabel_tolerance argument (a value of 0.5 generally provides a good compromise between legibility and location fidelity of node labels).

3.3.1 Visual anchoring and network plotting

The printed size of node labels can be adjusted with the help of responselabel_size. The next line of code shows how we added an informative title to the output plot with the plot_title argument. Finally, determining a particular numeric random seed with the seed argument ensures 1:1 replicability of the force-directed network layout if desired. Figure 4 presents the graphical component of the ResIN output.

3.3.2 Spatial interpretation, node-level covariates, and individual-level scores

In this section, we hone in on the spatial embeddings within ResIN networks. Because ResIN arranges response nodes along the axes of greatest co-endorsement variance, the spatial location of attitude nodes can be read as an empirical ordering of positions along a dominant latent division. As discussed in Section 5, this ordering is a descriptive summary of co-endorsement structure rather than an estimate of a latent variable in the measurement-theoretic sense, and its substantive interpretation should therefore be externally validated. We offer one such approach in the remainder of this section. The node location on the main spatial axis can be accessed via ResIN_out$ResIN_nodeframe$ x. To make the above hypotheses testable, we add respondents' own partisan self-identification (partisan) and their dislike of the Democratic party relative to their dislike of the Republican party (dem_bias) to the working dataset in the code snippet below. To ease interpretation, we recoded partisan attachments to fall within the unit interval, with democratic affiliation coded as 0 (more blue), Independents as 0.5, and Republicans at 1 (more red). To differentiate our co-variates from the actual issue position attitudes we wish to make inferences about, we can simply supply character vectors to the node_vars and node_covars arguments of the ResIN() function. Furthermore, since we would like to have access to the means of our two co-variates at the response node level, we need to specify node_costats=c("mean","mean"). This argument tells ResIN to obtain the node-level mean from each co-variate (hence the double call of "mean"). Other node_costats possibilities include the median (node_costats="median"), standard deviation ("sd"), or variance ("var"). To further enhance the visual output, we select the red-blue color palette (color_palette="RdBu") and reverse the direction with direction=-1 to ensure that item nodes endorsed by more Democrats appear in blue and Republicans in red.

The stored ResIN_out$ResIN_nodeframe object contains both node-level co-statistics, i.e., the average partisan endorsement (partisan_mean) and the affective bias against the Democratic party (dem_bias_mean) as attested to by our summary() calls below. In resulting Figure 5, we opted to visualize the former co-statistic with the aid of the plot_whichstat= "partisan_mean" argument. The color legend at the bottom of the resulting ggplot-based visualization is automatically populated based on that input as well.

Items_plus_Covs <-Core_Items %>% dplyr::mutate( partisan = as.numeric(recode(BrJSocPsychol_2024$Q13, "Democrat" = 0, "Independent" = 0.5, "Republican" = 1)))

Items_plus_Covs$dem_bias <-as.numeric(BrJSocPsychol_2024$Q15_2)as.numeric(BrJSocPsychol_2024$Q15_1)

ResIN_out <-ResIN(Items_plus_Covs, node_vars = c("legal_abort", "equalize_incomes", "keep_immigrants", "welfare_spending", "gay_marriage", "protect_environ", "gun_control", "aid_blacks"), node_covars = c("dem_bias", "partisan"), node_costats = c("mean", "mean"), plot_whichstat = "partisan_mean", left_anchor = "legal_abort_++", color_palette = "RdBu", direction = -1, plot_responselabels = FALSE, plot_title = "Node color reflects mean partisan endorsement", seed = 22)

Node color reflects average partisan endorsement The visual correspondence of average partisan endorsement and node location along the x-axis in Figure 5 provides initial confirmation that ResIN indeed captures a strong partisan division within the co-endorsement patterns in the political attitude nodes. Below, we additionally correlate the x-axis node positions with the node-level means of partisanship and negative partisan affect using the corr.test() function from the psych package to assess the strength of each pairwise association (Revelle, 2025).
