The probability values adjusted for multiple tests are in the p.adj object. [1] 0 The Pearson correlations of the node location in the latent space with partisanship and party-affective bias, 0.87 and 0.89 respectively, confirm our earlier visual impression: ResIN innately captures some of the essence of the partisan-ideological division among the American public.

We have just shown that there is a strong statistical relationship between response nodes and average partisanship per node. Yet, what about survey respondents themselves? How strong is this association at the level of respondents? ResIN() is capable of obtaining scores for all survey-takers by averaging the node positions among the attitude nodes that a given individual endorsed. These scores are stored in an n × 2 data-frame called ResIN_out$ResIN_scores; the major axis scores appear as raw_x and the minor axis component as raw_y.

ResIN() also obtains two further types of score estimates which are based on partial pooling of item and individual level means. First, the package implements a simple heuristic approach, heur_x, which weighs node-level means by their relative popularity. Second, a more principled strategy centered around empirical Bayesian shrinkage (Efron & Morris, 1973) imposes a datadriven penalty on both sparsely populated and overly extreme estimates (eb_x). We recommend either of these options if users experience large discrepancies in item response popularity or if there is substantial missingness among the individual-level data. For the sake of illustration, we correlate the empirical-Bayes scores with individual-level partisan bias below. The probability values adjusted for multiple tests are in the p.adj object.

[1] 0 Although somewhat smaller in magnitude, the above code output suggests that ResIN is capable of recovering a considerable association (r ≈ 0.7) between participants' ResIN -derived latent space scores and their partisan-affective bias.

3.3.3 Bootstrapping uncertainty

Above, we have shown that ResIN -derived attitude-node locations are strongly associated with respondents' average partisan identities. But what about statistical uncertainty? How can we be sure that this finding is robust to both sampling and other sources of error, particularly with respect to the additional uncertainty introduced by ResIN 's stochastic force-directed algorithm? Based on our running example, we showcase a simple workflow example for how to initiate, execute, and visualize bootstrapped uncertainty estimates around the above-obtained correlation coefficient between x-axis node location and political partisanship. Appendix A.3.2 covers a more thorough exposition of the ResIN bootstrapping verbs and their arguments. In brief, we first use the ResIN_boots_prepare() function to declare the ResIN-object we'd like to bootstrap and to determine the basic kind of bootstrapping procedure (i.e., boots_type="resample" to draw uncertainty bounds around parameters of interest, or boots_type="permute" to generate permutation-test null-hypothesis distributions). Second, we use the ResIN_boots_execute() function to carry out the planned bootstrapping procedure (optionally leveraging CPU parallelism with parallel=TRUE). Third and finally, the ResIN_boots_extract() function can be used to extract, concatenate, and optionally provide statistical summaries of the desired, resampled parameters.

Sticking with the analyses of the previous section, we will use the bootstrapping function to re-estimate the same ResIN -network with the mean-level of respondents' partisan identities as node-level co-variate of interest. To this end, we first re-specify a more bare-bones call to ResIN() which features only the arguments we need for this specific task, i.e., node_covars=c("partisan") and node_costats=c("mean"). Importantly, we disable any superfluous feature: network_ stats=FALSE,generate_ggplot=FALSE,plot_ggplot=FALSE,ResIN_scores=FALSE,detect_ clusters=FALSE. We recommend disabling all unnecessary arguments as best practice in bootstrapping workloads, especially for larger networks and bootstrap loads (i.e., 1,000 and above iterations on most consumer-based computer hardware), as this will significantly reduce computational demand and estimation times.

Finally, please note that we kept the anchoring node (left_anchor="legal_abort_++") unchanged. As covered in Section 3.3.1, ResIN does not possess any pre-configured understanding of the substantive 'meaning' of resulting spatial layouts. Recall that due to the PCA-rotation of the resulting force-directed layout, ResIN -solutions can be randomly mirrored such that all ideologically right-leaning attitudes might appear on the left-hand side while all left-leaning attitudes might appear on the right-hand side of the resulting layout. 6 Defining a single, directional anchoring node that is highly indicative of the substantive meaning of the underlying latent (ideological) space therefore ensures consistency in the overall spatial orientation (and hence, substantive interpretation) across ResIN() runs. 7 ResIN_barebones <-ResIN(Items_plus_Covs, node_vars = c("legal_abort", "equalize_incomes", "keep_immigrants", "welfare_spending", "gay_marriage", "protect_environ", "gun_control", "aid_blacks"), node_covars = c("partisan"), node_costats = c("mean"), left_anchor = "legal_abort_++", network_stats = FALSE, generate_ggplot = FALSE, plot_ggplot = FALSE, ResIN_scores = FALSE, detect_clusters = FALSE) At this stage, we're ready to move to implementing the actual bootstrapping procedure.

Since we ultimately want to generate uncertainty estimates around the correlation coefficient of interest (r x;party = 0.87), we should specify boots_type="resample" inside the ResIN_boots_ prepare() function. We further set the parallel argument to TRUE and allocate all available cores to the task at hand with detect_cores=TRUE to take full advantage of parallelism inside ResIN_boots_execute(). We further set the seed_boots argument to ensure replicability of the entire bootstrapping procedure (and not just singular runs of ResIN() within each bootstrap iteration which are set with the regular seed argument). 8 6 ResIN() simply reverses the spatial mirror-effect for all 'mirrored' solutions if left_anchor is set. This check is performed independently within each bootstrap iteration, so that every resampled layout is brought into a common orientation before node positions are extracted and aggregated. Thus, setting the left_anchor argument is especially important for bootstrapping ResIN networks as random flips in the spatial orientation of the dominant axis could contaminate inferences based on the (average) spatial location of nodes -as in our present example.

7 This safeguard is only as reliable as the directional anchor itself. Because the correction is applied conditional on the estimated position of a single node, sampling error in that node's location does not remain local but propagates to the entire layout as a discrete, network-wide reflection. The relevant quantity is the anchor's expected distance from the layout centroid relative to its own resampling variability: the share of mis-oriented iterations is approximately Φ(-|µa|/σa), where Φ(•) denotes the standard normal distribution function and µa and σa denote the mean and standard deviation of the anchor's bootstrapped x-coordinate. This is why we do not recommend very sparsely endorsed response nodes as anchor candidates. As they enter the association matrix with imprecisely estimated correlations, they may forfeit most of their surviving edges once within-item and negative correlations are removed (Equation 5); weakly connected nodes are positioned near-arbitrarily by the Fruchterman-Reingold algorithm. As a rule of thumb, we therefore recommend anchoring on a node endorsed by at least 5% of the sample -a criterion our own anchor comfortably satisfies -and confirming that it occupies a clearly lateral position in the full-sample solution.

8 To provide some compute time benchmarks , the present example (ResIN consisting of 40 nodes based on ## Please adjust bootstrap n to meet inferential needs and computational headroom ResIN_prepped <-ResIN_boots_prepare(ResIN_barebones, n = 1000, boots_type = "resample", seed_boots = 42) ResIN_executed <-ResIN_boots_execute(ResIN_prepped, parallel = TRUE, detect_cores = TRUE) Once this is done, we can use ResIN_boots_extract() to retrieve and concatenate our target parameters of interest.

The extract function performs a forward search operation across all resampled networks, retrieving each object (typically vectors or scalars stored inside each ResINoutput list) exactly matching a particular search term. Thus, ResIN_boots_extract() typically returns a numbered list of the same length as the number of pre-specified bootstrap iterations. In our example, we first search the bootstrapped output list for what="x" to retrieve all xaxis node positions across all resampled networks (which the function compiles from all nested ResIN_out$ResIN_nodeframe$x vectors). In other words, ResIN_boots_extract(ResIN_ executed,what="x") searches our large bootstrap results list (ResIN_executed) for all instances of the x-axis coordinate locations (i.e., the content of each ResIN_out$ResIN_nodeframe$ x vector) which we conveniently store in a new list, called x_positions. Next, we retrieve respondents' average partisanship as our node-level covariate of interest. This is accomplished with the second call of ResIN_boots_extract(ResIN_executed,what="partisan_mean"). x_positions <-ResIN_boots_extract(ResIN_executed, what = "x") partisan_means <-ResIN_boots_extract(ResIN_executed, what = "partisan_mean") Since the objects extracted by ResIN_boots_extract() are stored as consecutively numbered lists, all we have to do is to generate a new list of the same length as our bootstrapped n = 1000 and subsequently fill the latter with the correlation estimates for each observed pair of partisan_means and x_positions. correlations <-list() for(i in 1:length(partisan_means)){ correlations[[i]] <-cor(partisan_means[[i]], x_positions[[i]])} correlations <-unlist(correlations) correlations <-as.data.frame(correlations) The final lines detail how we opted to quantify and visualize the resampled distribution. The 402 respondents at a bootstrap n of 1,000) takes approx. 35 sec. to compute in parallel-threaded and 49 sec. single-threaded on an 8-core AMD Ryzen 9 8945HS processor -a mid-tier, consumer-based laptop CPU in mid-2026. On a 16-core Ryzen 9 9955HX3D -a high-end consumer-grade CPU -we measured compute times at 9 sec. (parallel) and 25 sec. (single-core), respectively.

results in Figure 6 suggest that the correlation between partisanship and the spatial location of our attitude nodes achieves statistical significance at any conventional threshold.

3.4 Network statistics

ResIN() delivers several off-the-shelf estimates for node-level network statistics, including the strength, closeness, and betweenness-centrality, as well as expected node influence. The raw metrics for each node-level statistic are stored in ResIN_out$ResIN_nodeframe. The below code chunk shows how to visualize node-level differences in betweenness centrality; it is possible to set the argument plot_whichstat to either "Strength", "Closeness", "Betweenness", or "ExpectedInfluence". These statistics are derived from internal function calls to qgraph:: centrality_auto and DirectedClustering::ClustF which were customized for ResIN graphs (Clemente, 2024; Epskamp et al., 2012).

ResIN_out <-ResIN(Core_Items, plot_whichstat = "Betweenness", plot_responselabels = T, plot_title = "Node color based on betweenness centrality", color_palette = "PuOr", direction = -1, responselabel_repel = TRUE, responselabel_size = 3.2, responselabel_tolerance = 0.5, seed = 22, left_anchor = "legal_abort_++")

The output in Figure 7 shows that the "strongly agree with the legality of gay marriage" response possesses the highest betweenness centrality within the broader, left-wing attitude cluster. Descriptively, this indicates that the corresponding response node occupies a bridging position in the estimated layout, in the sense that a comparatively large share of the shortest paths linking the left and center-left clusters passes through it. One possible reading is that this attitude is co-endorsed across otherwise distinct belief communities and therefore represents a point of overlap between them. Betweenness centrality, however, is a descriptive property of the estimated co-endorsement structure rather than evidence about how attitudes are formed, communicated, or revised in cross-community dialogue. Because the underlying data are crosssectional and aggregated across respondents, a bridging position of this kind cannot -in and of itself -establish that the issue in question is in fact more persuasive, less contentious, or more conducive to cross-cutting dialogue than attitudes occupying more peripheral positions in the network. We suggest treating findings of this sort as hypothesis-generating rather than testing.

Descriptive network statistics can identify candidate attitudes whose cross-cluster standing may warrant closer examination with research designs capable of supporting confirmatory inference.

legal_abort_++ equalize_incomes_++ keep_immigrants_++ welfare_spending_++ protect_environ_++ gun_control_++ aid_blacks_++ protect_environ_-protect_environ_--legal_abort_+ keep_immigrants_+/-gay_marriage_--aid_blacks_-legal_abort_--equalize_incomes_+/-legal_abort_+/-gun_control_+ gun_control_+/-gay_marriage_-gun_control_-aid_blacks_--aid_blacks_+/-gay_marriage_+/-legal_abort_-welfare_spending_--gay_marriage_+ equalize_incomes_+ gun_control_--keep_immigrants_--equalize_incomes_-protect_environ_+/-aid_blacks_+ welfare_spending_+/-welfare_spending_-keep_immigrants_+ equalize_incomes_--keep_immigrants_-welfare_spending_+ protect_environ_+ gay_marriage_++ Betweenness 0 50 100 150 200 Node color based on betweenness centrality ResIN() also supports visual adjustments to the edge thickness on the basis of either the bivariate correlation weight (plot_edgestat="weight") or the edge betweenness centrality (plot_ Edge thickness based on edge-betweenness centrality Graph-level statistics, such as the link density and linearization -metrics of belief system constraint and polarization that were suggested by Chen et al., 2025 -can be accessed with ResIN_out$graph_stats[[1]]["link_density"] and ~["linearization"], respectively. By default, ResIN() also calculates the global clustering coefficient, average path length, and the network diameter. To turn this feature off, users need to specify network_stats=FALSE.

3.5 Cluster detection

ResIN() can directly call all cluster detection algorithms that are implemented in the igraph package (Csardi & Nepusz, 2006). To turn this feature on, simply set the detect_clusters argument to TRUE. By default, ResIN obtains latent cluster assignments based on the leading eigenvector method (cluster_method="cluster_leading_eigen"). These assignments can be accessed with ResIN_nodeframe$cluster and visualized with plot_whichstat="cluster", as shown in Figure 9. Individual-level, probabilistic cluster assignments can be found as part of the auxiliary output objects: ResIN_out$aux_objects$cluster_probabilities. Discrete, maximum probability assignments for each survey taker, meanwhile, are contained in the max_clusterprob object stored in the same location. The code snippet below reproduces Figure 9 and shows how to access the information on cluster assignments.

ResIN_out <-ResIN(Core_Items, detect_clusters = TRUE, plot_whichstat = "cluster", plot_responselabels = FALSE, plot_title = "Leading eigenvalue community detection", color_palette = "Set2", seed = 22, left_anchor = "legal_abort_++")

ResIN_out$ResIN_nodeframe %>% select(node_names, cluster) %>% head() Description:df [6 × 2] node_names <chcluster <dbl> 1 legal_abort_-1 2 legal_abort_--3 3 legal_abort_+ 1 4 legal_abort_+/-3 5 legal_abort_++ 2 6 equalize_incomes_-1 1-6 of 6 rows Cluster (Leading Eigen) Nodes per cluster:1=17;2=8;3=12;4=3 1 2 3 4

Leading eigenvalue community detection that the moderate node communities (1 and 4 above and 1 below) appear spatially more proximate to the right-wing cluster, which suggests a more similar answering behavior among these respondent groups.

3.6 Network pruning

ResIN features two approaches to enhance network sparsity, i.e., the pruning of weak and (likely) idiosyncratic edges.

The first is based on raw p-value thresholds. Users can specify remove_nonsignificant=TRUE which calls psych::corr.test() to perform pairwise t-tests on the individual correlation coefficients (Revelle, 2025). If remove_nonsignificant=TRUE is set, ResIN() forces to zero all edge-weights whose p-values exceed the default threshold of p < 0.05; this threshold can be altered with the sign_threshold argument.

The second approach implements false discovery rate (FDR) control via the Benjamini-Hochberg step-up procedure (Benjamini & Hochberg, 1995). Concretely, when remove_nonsignificant_ method="fdr", ResIN() first obtains edge-wise p-values (for the set of unique node pairs) and then applies a flexible adjustment across this family of tests (implemented via stats::p. adjust(method="BH")).

Edges are retained if their adjusted p-values fall below sign_threshold, which is interpreted as the target FDR level q; all remaining edges are pruned by setting their weights to zero. Relative to a fixed per-edge threshold (e.g., p < 0.05), this approach adapts the rejection boundary to the global distribution of p-values and controls the expected proportion of false discoveries among retained edges, typically yielding greater power while maintaining a principled error rate (Benjamini & Yekutieli, 2001).

Pruning ResIN -networks can have benefits and drawbacks. On the one hand, removing statistically insignificant edges can yield simpler structures which are easier to interpret and more likely to generalize to the target population of interest (Epskamp & Fried, 2018)

3.7 Multimodal networks

As of package version 2.3.1., ResIN() is capable of modeling attitude networks as multimodal graphs in which binary response nodes are connected via respondents who co-endorse the same attitudes (Quayle, 2025). Such graphs co-locate people and their socially shared beliefs within the same network structure, treating the implicit connections explicitly, in the form of quasior para-social networks (Dinkelberg et al., 2021). Multi-modal ResIN -networks thus enable community-detection and network centrality-based investigations at the level of individual survey takers, leading to more direct inferences about clustering and belief system centrality of individuals within a shared attitude space.

To generate such networks, users can simply set multimodal=T. By default, ResIN() determines the approximate location of each respondent within the basic, response-node graph, using the same force-directed method described above. Crucially, the positions of the response nodes are held constant while respondent nodes freely circulate until a force equilibrium is found.

ResIN() outputs a 'node-frame' type data-frame, called coordinate_df which contains the x and y axis coordinates found for individual participants and a plot-able ggraph-object called multimodal_ggraph. In the example in Figure 13, we further specified a cluster detection task to be performed on (both the regular ResIN and) the multimodal network variant. ResIN() automatically calculates and returns the sum of participant and response nodes belonging to each of the obtained clusters. In the code snippet below, we show how to use the customized S3 method (plot.ResIN()) for plotting the multimodal graph using the which argument.

ResIN_out <-ResIN(Core_Items, node_vars = c("legal_abort", "equalize_incomes", "keep_immigrants", "welfare_spending", "gay_marriage", "protect_environ", "gun_control", "aid_blacks"), multimodal = TRUE, detect_clusters = TRUE, plot_whichstat = "cluster", left_anchor = "legal_abort_++", plot_ggplot = FALSE, seed =22) plot(ResIN_out, which = "multimodal")

Cluster (Leading Eigen) P/R per cluster:1=144/27;2=180/8;3=78/5 1 2 3 Node type Participant Response node Multimodal ResIN graph Figure 13: Multimodal ResIN plot with fixed attitude and variable respondent nodes. Here, community detection (based on leading eigenvalue structures) operates directly on respondent and attitude nodes, lending a more direct inference about community memberships among survey takers.

3.8 Export to other software environments

Finally, users who are familiar with other graph-based software environments can leverage one of several as-based S3-class conversion methods shipping with ResIN; as.igraph and as.qgraph, for instance, convert the output of the ResIN() function into native igraph (Csardi & Nepusz, 2006) and qgraph-objects (Epskamp et al., 2012), respectively. The as.gephi method exports the ResIN edge list into a compact csv-file with variable names that are readable by Gephi.

& Sleegers, 2021; Dalege et al., 2016; Marsman et al., 2018). 9 Item-level partial-correlation approaches, such as belief network analysis and the Gaussian graphical model, apply the same conditional dependence logic to (quasi-)continuous indicators (Boutyline & Vaisey, 2017; Brandt et al., 2019; Epskamp & Fried, 2018; Warncke, 2025). Exploratory graph analysis (EGA) builds on the latter to estimate the number of latent dimensions underlying a set of items (H. F. Golino & Epskamp, 2017), and latent space item response models (LSIRM) embed respondents and items in a shared metric space in order to recover the dependence that a conventional measurement model leaves unexplained (Jeon et al., 2021; Kang & Jeon, 2024). The latter thereby formally extend the latent space tradition in social network analysis (Hoff et al., 2002) to item response data. 10ResIN differs from all of these in three main respects. As summarized in Table 1, ResINnodes represent individual item responses rather than items, ResIN -edges encode marginal (zero-order) co-endorsement rather than conditional dependence, and the method's primary inferential object is the geometry of the resulting layout rather than the structure implied by the adjacency matrix. 11 We consider each in turn and take up the practical limitations that follow from them in Section 5. Perhaps the most consequential difference concerns the primary unit of analysis in each network method. Because every response option enters the network as a dedicated node, ResIN models network structures that item-level methods cannot represent by construction: the two poles of a single item may occupy quite different positions within the latent space. Similarly, 'neutral' or 'don't know' answers are not necessarily forced into the center (or periphery) of estimated attitude networks but may obtain unique, structurally and theoretically meaningful positions across the belief system space (see, e.g., Carpentras et al., 2022; Chen et al., 2025; Warncke et al., 2026). Furthermore, by operationalizing nodes as item-responses, ResIN is capable of revealing potential asymmetries in the organization of belief-systems, thus making ResIN a particularly fruitful approach for investigating belief systems at the group level (e.g., as exemplified by Lüders et al., 2024).

ResIN 's more fine-grained resolution also changes the meaning of node-level descriptive statistics. Node strength in a GGM approximates the total unique association of a node within its local network environment, and thus its relative embeddedness or structural importancea property conceptually related to Converse's (1964) notion of static belief system centrality.

Node strength in ResIN, by contrast, sums positive marginal correlations and therefore reflects the popularity of individual response options, a property closer to localized, opinion-based group prototypicality (Quayle, 2025). ResIN -derived node statistics (Section 3.4) are consequently not interchangeable with their conditional-dependence counterparts.

The same holds for communities (Section 3.5). EGA detects communities in a regularized partial-correlation network and interprets them as the dimensions underlying a set of items (H. F. Golino & Epskamp, 2017). Communities in ResIN -graphs instead group mutually coendorsed response options, and can therefore distribute the response scale of a single item across two or more clusters. That behavior is impossible by construction in item-level EGA, and is substantively informative in its own right, since it locates the point on a response continuum at which a position ceases to belong to one attitudinal camp and begins to belong to another. Furthermore, because ResIN processes every data point at the nominal level of measurement, these communities of mutually endorsed responses can include non-numeric response options (e.g., "Don't know/prefer not to say") or even observational or behavioral metadata.

A second difference concerns the quantity that edges encode. In a Gaussian graphical model, the Ising model, or a more general Markov random field, an edge represents a conditional association, i.e., the residual dependence remaining between two variables once every other variable in the system has been accounted for. The absence of an edge is correspondingly informative, since it constitutes an explicit claim of conditional independence. This property licenses reading such graphs as networks of direct interactions between attitude elements (Brandt & Sleegers, 2021; Dalege et al., 2016; Marsman et al., 2018). ResIN -derived edges, by contrast, rest on unconditional, homophilic co-endorsement patterns (Carpentras et al., 2024; Quayle, 2025).

ResIN networks thus carry limited information about the extent to which co-endorsement is driven by direct rather than indirect influence between any given pair of attitudes. Nevertheless, the statistical pipelines of other belief network methods can mask features exposed by ResIN, for example, attitude asymmetries between groups (e.g., Carpentras et al., 2024; Chen et al., 2025). This contrast is especially instructive in the case of the CAN model, which shares with ResIN an interest in the spatial arrangement of attitudinal elements but differs in what nodes and edges are taken to represent. Nodes in CAN-networks represent evaluative reactions toward a single attitude object, and CAN-edges are intended as direct causal connections within an individual's cognitive system, such that the estimated network is assumed isomorphic with the attitude construct itself (Dalege et al., 2016). ResIN -nodes represent response options spanning distinct issue items, and ResIN -edges summarize population-level co-endorsement patterns. A CAN-edge accordingly supports claims about how one evaluative reaction constrains another within a person, while a ResIN -edge captures which positions travel together across people (Quayle, 2025).

The third difference follows from the above. Because individual edges carry little interpretive weight on their own, the object of inference shifts from the adjacency matrix to the layout it generates. The collection of retained edges forms a global co-endorsement structure whose force equilibrium can reveal the major divisions between groups of respondents holding similar views. ResIN -based analyses accordingly focus on the overall geometry rather than on individual links. Spatial distances are a property of that layout rather than a directly estimated model parameter, which is what makes the bootstrapping routines of Section 3.3.3, rather than closedform standard errors, the appropriate instrument for quantifying uncertainty.

Among the methods considered here, latent space item response models bear the closest formal resemblance to ResIN, and that resemblance is closest of all to the multimodal extension introduced in Section 3.7. Both approaches take item response data as input, embed the resulting entities in a shared low-dimensional metric space, interpret that space in terms of proximity, locate respondents alongside items or responses, and support clustering of the resulting configuration (Jeon et al., 2021; Kang & Jeon, 2024). Conceptually, both also inherit the latent space tradition in social network analysis (Hoff et al., 2002), in which the probability of a tie declines in the distance between the positions of the two units involved.

The substantive meaning of the latent network spaces produced by ResIN and LSIRM is nonetheless close to opposite. The LSIRM augments a Rasch model with a distance term, such that the log odds of an affirmative response are a function of a person's ability θ p , an item's easiness b k , and a penalty -γd(ξ p , ζ k ). In other words, the method renders a residual network space that represents the conditional dependence that remains once the main person and item effects have been accounted for (Jeon et al., 2021; Kang & Jeon, 2024). A dominant left-right dimension of the kind ResIN is designed to recover would, in an LSIRM, be absorbed almost entirely by θ p and b k , and would consequently be largely invisible on the interaction map. 12In summary, ResIN is likely to be the most appropriate methodological choice when • the object of theoretical interest concerns homophilic co-endorsement patterns (i.e., which positions travel together in a population, and how the resulting constellations are spatially arranged) • asymmetries across the response options of an item are substantively meaningful • a communicable, spatially interpretable representation that co-locates people and positions is required • the analyst is unwilling to commit in advance to latent-variable models with strong assumptions about latent dimensionality, linearity, or group-based symmetries • item non-response or qualitative (e.g., write-in text) responses are of central research interest and should be included in the estimated network Conditional-dependence models are preferable when researchers are specifically interested in the distinction between direct and indirect node associations, or when the analysis is intended to feed a dynamical model of belief change.

For such purposes, the Ising and Markov random field literatures supply both the theory and the estimation methods (Brandt & Sleegers, 2021; Marsman et al., 2018), with IsingFit (van Borkulo et al., 2014), mgm (Haslbeck & Waldorp, 2020)-which accommodates nominal categorical variables and thus comes closest to a conditional-dependence analogue of response-level nodes-and the Bayesian bgms (Marsman et al., 2025) available in R, alongside qgraph and bootnet for Gaussian graphical models (Epskamp & Fried, 2018; Epskamp et al., 2012). EGA, implemented in EGAnet (H. Golino & Christensen, 2026; H. F. Golino & Epskamp, 2017), is a more appropriate tool when the core research question deals with latent dimensionality, and the LSIRM, implemented in lsirm12pl (Go et al., 2025), when it concerns residual person-item interactions. These methodological choices are by no means mutually exclusive, and we encourage readers to treat ResIN as one BNM among several others. The export methods documented in Section 3.8 are intended to make such combinations straightforward: a ResIN graph can be inspected in qgraph or igraph using the same tooling an analyst would apply to a GGM, and the item-level (Go et al., 2025), whereas ResIN operates on response options and neither requires nor exploits an assumption that these are ordered. Third, the methods therefore answer different questions: researchers asking whether particular respondents interact idiosyncratically with particular items-a differential item functioning question posed without having to specify the grouping variable in advance-are likely better served by an LSIRM, for which the lsirm12pl package provides a full R implementation (Go et al., 2025).

data underlying it can be passed directly to EGAnet or bgms. Similarly, the individual-level scores described in Section 3.3.2 can be validated against factor or item response scores estimated on the same battery.

5 Limitations, practical recommendations, and ongoing work

In this last section, we summarize some principal limitations users should anticipate, along with our recommendations for working around them, and close with current efforts to address the most fundamental among them.

ResIN 's response-level resolution comes at the price of graph size and complexity. Because the node set grows multiplicatively in the number of items, visual legibility and inferential precision can degrade well before they would for item-level alternatives, and researchers working with large item batteries should consider restricting the analysis to a theoretically motivated subset of items, pruning the network (Section 3.6), or collapsing response categories. We should stress that the last of these entails a particularly substantive modeling decision: collapsing a seven-point scale to five or three points alters the node set, the cell frequencies underlying every correlation, and hence the estimated layout. We recommend reporting the sensitivity of substantive conclusions to at least one alternative categorization.

ResIN also relies on adequate sample sizes and-more importantly-co-endorsement frequencies. Each entry of Z (see Equation 4) is estimated from a 2 × 2 contingency table, so the binding constraint is typically not the overall sample size but the joint endorsement frequency of any given pair of response options. 13 Sparsely endorsed nodes may therefore enter Z with imprecisely estimated correlations and be positioned quite arbitrarily by the Fruchterman and Reingold, 1991 algorithm. We recommend inspecting the marginal endorsement distribution before estimation and reporting bootstrapped uncertainty for any node-level or spatial quantity on which a substantive claim rests (cf. Epskamp & Fried, 2018).

At the edge level, the principal simplification is the clipping of negative correlations (Equation 5). Because the response options of a given item are mutually exclusive, the largest negative entries in Z are typically those between opposite poles of the same item, and these are already zeroed by the within-item restriction. What clipping additionally removes are cross-item repul-sions, that is, pairs of positions endorsed together less frequently than chance would predict.

In structured attitude spaces this information is largely redundant, since the layout expresses repulsion through the constant repulsive force and the absence of attraction, and Carpentras et al., 2024 report no appreciable loss in the recovery of node positions. That redundancy is not guaranteed to hold in every application, however, and the extent to which clipping alters the resulting geometry will depend on endorsement frequencies, response sparsity, and the dimensional structure of the data. 14 Two properties of the layout itself call for further attention. First, the Fruchterman-Reingold algorithm is stochastic, and its solutions are identified only up to reflection along the dominant axis; we therefore highly recommend fixing an informative directional anchor node (Section 3.3.1). Second, because spatial distances are not estimated parameters, they carry no analytic standard errors. Bootstrapping (Section 3.3.3) provides the primary strategy for quantifying uncertainty around spatial quantities obtained with ResIN. 15 Another limitation stems from the fact that ResIN is not, as of this writing, derived from or based in a formal measurement theory. Unlike the Ising model, which has been shown to stand in direct correspondence with multidimensional item response theory (Marsman et al., 2018), or the LSIRM discussed above, ResIN specifies no likelihood, no explicit data-generating process, and no formal criterion for model comparison. 16 Analysts who require formal parametric testssuch as edge-inclusion tests or Bayes factors-are thus better served by an alternative with an explicit foundation in probability theory.

However, work aimed at closing this gap is currently under way. Carpentras et al., 2027 develop Belief Network Theory as an explicit measurement framework for complex identity belief systems, with the aim of supplying models of this type with a formal foundation in a 14 A systematic sensitivity analysis across these conditions would require a dedicated simulation study and lies beyond the scope of the present software tutorial. The ResIN package nevertheless supplies the requisite tools: ResIN_boots_prepare(boots_type="resample") returns confidence bounds around node coordinates, boots_type="permute" generates null distributions against which observed spatial structure can be compared (Section 3.3.3), and the significance-based pruning routines of Section 3.6 allow the retained edge set to be varied. Where negatively signed relations are themselves the object of interest (e.g., Brandt & Sleegers, 2021), a signed conditional-dependence model is the more appropriate instrument, and the unthresholded association matrix remains recoverable from aux_objects [["df_dummies"]] for direct comparison. 15 We should add that the restriction to mapping network structures into two dimensions is a visualization and interpretability choice in the current implementation rather than the outcome of a formal dimensionality criterion (Section 2.4). In theory, however, ResIN works in an arbitrary number of latent dimensions. 16 Although there is no formal link between item-response theory and ResIN, Carpentras et al., 2024 conducted extensive simulations showing a near 1:1 correspondence between the item-response node locations on the dominant axis estimated with ResIN and the shape of item-response curves under various (unidimensional) IRT-based data-generation processes. The method thus faithfully reconstructs the influence of dominant latent variables if they are noticeable at the data level.

latent network-based theory of measurement. We regard the integration of ResIN into such a framework, together with a systematic simulation study of the clipping, sparsity, and dimensionality questions raised above, as the most valuable directions for the further development of the method.

6 Conclusion

This paper provides an overview of ResIN for R. The package was designed with a 'one-stop shop' philosophy to facilitate end-to-end implementation of the ResIN method. Beyond implementing the main computational algorithms, ResIN() produces various outputs that researchers are typically interested in. These include co-variate statistics and individual-level position scores (Section 3.3.2), bootstrapped uncertainty bounds around these (Section 3.3.3), various network descriptive statistics (Section 3.4), and community detection (Section 3.5). We also discussed more advanced methods and tools, such as network pruning (Section 3.6), generating multimodal structures (Section 3.7), and export to other statistical programming environments (Section 3.8).

While ResIN was first computationally introduced in the Python language (Carpentras et al., 2024), a comprehensive software suite centered around the method did not exist prior to the present implementation in R. 17 Researchers interested in making use of existing Python, C++, or Mathematica libraries for advanced network inference and visualization can, however, rely on the integrated igraph interface to import ResIN -graph objects generated in R into their desired language ecosystem, via the ResIN::as.igraph.ResIN() method.

ResIN will continue to evolve; news about additional features, bug fixes, and interesting applications are periodically shared on the official method website, https://www.resinmethod.net/. Users are actively encouraged to submit bug reports at https://github.com/pwarncke77/ResIN/issues and request new features by contacting the corresponding author.

Equation ( 17) preserves all pairwise distances but removes arbitrary rotation from the Fruchterman-Reingold result.

A.3 Computational implementation of main functions in R

A.3.1 Flagship function: ResIN()

The ResIN() function implements all components of the ResIN -method as covered in Section 2. First, it takes on the dummy transformation task (see Equation 2) by calling dummy_cols() from the fastDummies package (Kaplan & Schlegel, 2025). Next, ResIN() derives the association structure among response nodes directly from the dummy response matrix using a unified correlation backend. By default, the function obtains Pearson correlations (via stats::cor) from the dummy matrix, with optional support for both list-wise and pairwise handling of partial missingness. When a survey-weight vector is provided with the weights argument (either as a dedicated object or via a column name indicating the weights variable within the supplied data-frame), ResIN() instead obtains weighted correlations. In this case, the pair-specific effective sample size under unequal weights is obtained from Kish's design-effect formulation (Kish, 1965). 18 If users enable p-value-based network pruning (i.e., by setting remove_nonsignificant=TRUE), the function additionally produces uncertainty estimates for Pearson correlations to support edge filtering (either via raw p-values or Benjamini-Hochberg false-discovery rate adjustment (Benjamini & Hochberg, 1995)). Specifying cor_method="tetrachoric" instead calls psych::tetrachoric (Revelle, 2025) which can be helpful for users working with zero-inflated item-response data.

ResIN() subsequently sets all within-item correlations to zero with a simple while-loop that also clips off all remaining negative correlations. The function then passes the correlation matrix to the igraph package implementation of the Fruchterman-Reingold algorithm, using the layout_with_fr() function (Csardi & Nepusz, 2006). This function was chosen because of its flexibility and speed, as it is based on an efficient C++ backend (see Appendix A.1 for details). The resulting bi-dimensional layout is rotated such that the dominant axis corresponds with the x-dimension by calling stats::prcomp() which simultaneously implements Equations 15 through 17.

If no further user input is supplied, ResIN() generates a basic labeled plot of the resulting network structure with the aid of ggplot2 (Wickham et al., 2019), along with a list of network estimation results in both a long-format edge-list (ResIN_edgelist), and a wide-format nodelevel dataframe (ResIN_nodeframe) -each of which contains the rotated network layout. The function further produces basic, positional score estimates for individual survey participants, the implementation of which, including customization options, is further described in Section 3.3.2. Finally, a set of graph-level descriptive statistics (e.g., global clustering, average path length, network diameter), is obtained via various function calls to qgraph (Epskamp et al., 2012) and igraph (Csardi & Nepusz, 2006) and stored in the graph_stats output list (see Section 3.4). We cover the implementation of all advanced capabilities that require further input arguments to ResIN(), such as cluster detection (Section 3.5), edge pruning (Section 3.6), and the generation of bi-partite, respondent-attitude networks (Section 3.7) further below.

A.3.2 Bootstrapping functions

In general, statistical quantities of interest generated with ResIN -graphs cannot rely on closedform probability distributions to derive uncertainty bounds. Statistical inference therefore needs to rely on non-parametric bootstrapping procedures (Costantini et al., 2015). Unfortunately, such procedures can be taxing -computationally and programming-wise -especially for users with limited statistical coding experience. This is why ResIN provides a series of helper functions that were designed to simplify and accelerate the front-end programming, back-end computation, and downstream data extraction and visualization tasks associated with generating and evaluating statistical uncertainty. More specifically, bootstrapping workflow with ResIN involves three steps, each of which is accomplished with a dedicated helper function: 1.

ResIN_boots_prepare() readies a given ResIN-class object for re-sampling tasks. The additional parameters of this function, n and boots_type, determine the number of iterations and the basic kind of bootstrapping procedure users wish to perform. Supported options for the latter argument are "resample" and "permute". The former implements row-wise resampling of data rows with replacement and should be used to generate confidence intervals around particular point estimates. This mode relies on the sample_n function provided by dplyr (Wickham et al., 2019). The boots_type="permute" option prescribes a permutation test by distorting all the entries in the original data-matrix, X (which is implemented with a nested for loop which simultaneously "re-organizes" both data rows and columns via the base::sample() function). This procedure derives zerocentered, null-hypothesis distributions around parameters of interest. In both cases, the resample_size argument determines sample size within each bootstrap iteration which can be useful in statistical power-and sensitivity analyses. 19 Survey weights can be supplied as a numeric weights vector of the same length as the original dataset, using the weights argument. This step ensures that resampling follows demographic properties of the target population. A few sensible guardrails hard-coded in ResIN_boots_prepare() prevent the automatic console transfer of n re-estimated network plots during the bootstrapping procedure.

2. ResIN_boots_execute() takes the output generated by ResIN_boots_prepare() and implements the pre-defined bootstrapping procedure. If the parallel option is set to TRUE, the function exploits CPU parallelism via the doSNOW engine (Daniel et al., 2022). Users can supply the number of desired workers by setting the n_cores argument to the desired integer value. If detect_cores is set to TRUE, all available CPU cores detected by parallel::detectCores() will be employed; this number can also be offset with the core_offset argument which reduces the number of workers by the supplied integer value. Essentially, ResIN_boots_execute() calls n instances of the ResIN() function, each supplied with the instructions generated by ResIN_boots_prepare().

3. Once the re-estimation procedure has concluded, ResIN_boots_extract() provides a search-and-grab utility to locate and summarize the output list generated by ResIN_ boots_execute() for particular quantities of interest. Users solely need to specify a search term with the what argument, e.g., what="x" for bootstrapped node positions along the dominant ResIN axis. The function grabs, concatenates, and returns every object (typically vectors or scalar values depending on the search term) across all bootstrapped networks that matches the search term. 20 By default, ResIN_boots_extract() returns an enumerated list object containing all detected elements. By setting summarize_results= TRUE the function alternatively summarizes the distribution over the extracted objects across all bootstrap iterations via stats::quantile(). By default, the summarize_results option returns the minimum, maximum, probability quantiles (i.e., 2.5th, 5th, 25th, 50th,

This function was created on the basis of Hogervorst, 2022's gephi package. The code chunk below illustrates the usage of these methods.

4 Situating ResIN among related belief network models

ResIN not only shares much of its conceptual and operational scope with a rapidly expanding family of BNMs, but also possesses a number of stand-alone qualities that users should weigh when choosing among available approaches. This section attempts to briefly situate the method (as implemented in package version 2.3.1) relative to other popular BNMs with available software implementations in R. While a systematic methods comparison lies well beyond the scope of a package tutorial, our aim here is to provide basic guidance on whether ResIN is an appropriate instrument for a given research task.

Most established belief-and attitude-network models estimate conditional associations between (survey-)items. The Ising network model and its relatives represent attitudes as binary states whose pairwise couplings are estimated after conditioning on all remaining nodes (Brandt

Appendix A.1 Implementation of Fruchterman-Reingold algorithm

First, as shown in Equation ( 9), we interpret the non-negative correlation matrix Z ′ as a weighted graph G whose vertices correspond to response nodes and whose edges carry the positive weights w uv.

Second, we set the global length scale k that balances attraction and repulsion. It depends only on the drawing area A and the number of vertices s.

Third, following Fruchterman and Reingold, 1991, repulsive forces decay inversely with distance, while attractive forces grow quadratically; these simple laws keep dense parts compact without letting the layout collapse.

Fourth, at every iteration t we compute the net force ∆ (t) u acting on vertex u by summing repulsion from all other vertices and weighted attraction from its neighbors.

Fifth, each vertex is displaced in the direction of its net force but by no more than the current "temperature" T t , which gradually cools by a factor α to promote convergence.

Finally, after T iterations the columns of the matrix C contain the two-dimensional coordinates that embed the s response nodes in the plane.

A.2 Principal component coordinate rotation

To perform the coordinate rotation, we first translate the layout matrix C so its centroid sits at the origin:

Next, we eigen-decompose the 2 × 2 covariance matrix of the centered coordinates-its eigenvectors form an orthogonal rotation that aligns the axes with the principal directions:

Finally, applying that rotation yields the principal-component-oriented layout C ′ :

A core strength of the ResIN method lies in producing spatially interpretable networks, e.g., in terms of the familiar left-right political spectrum. It is therefore good practice to nudge the spatial orientation of ResIN -networks by forcing at least one easily interpretable anchor node to appear on either the right-hand or left-hand side of the latent attitude space.

Figure 4: ResIN network generated from eight political attitude questions fielded by Lüders et al., 2024. Created with ggplot2

Following Lüders et al., 2024's hypothesis, if the left-right location of the abstract network space correlates substantially with partisan identities and animosities in the United States, there should be strong reason to believe that ResIN -networks can spatially capture the familiar liberal-conservative attitude spectrum.

The Causal Attitude Network (CAN) model(Dalege et al., 2016) provides a substantive theory built on this formalism, conceptualizing an attitude as a network of evaluative reactions whose dynamics conform to an Ising model and whose parameters are recovered through regularized neighborhood selection.

The multimodal ResIN extension (Section 3.7) is the closest point of contact between the approaches, since it likewise places respondents and response nodes within a single spatial representation. Both treat a two-mode structure-units on one side, items or positions on the other-and interpret proximity as a higher propensity for the corresponding tie. The main difference lies in what the coordinates represent. In the Hoff et al., 2002 latent network space approach, latent positions are parameters of a probability model for tie formation, jointly estimated for all units and reported with posterior uncertainty. In multimodal ResIN, by contrast, endorsement ties are observed rather than modeled, and respondent coordinates are obtained by holding the response-node layout fixed and relaxing respondents to a force equilibrium within it. Respondent positions therefore inherit the attitude geometry rather than co-determine it, which keeps estimation fast and the resulting scores directly interpretable, but forfeits the model-based uncertainty that the latent space tradition supplies.

Unlike any established BNM, ResIN can treat every type of response as nominal and can hence include nonnumeric response options such as refusal ("Don't know/prefer not to say", "Question not asked"), open-ended responses, and various types of discrete metadata as dedicated nodes within the estimated network.

Three further differences matter in practice. First, the LSIRM is estimated by MCMC, which delivers posterior uncertainty for every position and a spike-and-slab test of whether a latent space is warranted at all, at the computational cost of Bayesian estimation; ResIN imposes none of these requirements and typically returns a solution in seconds, but forgoes closed-form, model-based inference. Second, the LSIRM operates on items, with dichotomous responses in its base specification and extensions to two-parameter and continuous responses

In our running example (n ≈ 400, eight items, five options), an approximately uniform response distribution implies an expected count of only 400 × 0.2 ×0.2 ≈ 16 in the cell in which both options are endorsed; for substantially skewed items and rarely selected options, the effective cell counts are considerably smaller.

A tutorial for implementing the method in Python can be found here: https://www.resinmethod.net/python-0/

ResIN relies on the svyweight package(Mainwaring, 2022) for the implementation of Kish's design-effect.

This option defaults to the number of rows in the raw data matrix, X.

ResIN_boots_extract() only finds and returns exact matches. Thus, users need to know the specific object name of the quantity produced by the ResIN() function. For instance, what="eb_x" returns empirical-Bayes shrinkage adjusted scores for individual survey respondents, whereas what="x" retrieves the node locations along the major coordinate axis of the ResIN layout. All named objects of the ResIN() function are unique, avoiding conflict between potential search terms.
